Here is my detailed summary of findings for each tool.

---

## 1. jj-fzf

**Repository:** [github.com/tim-janik/jj-fzf](https://github.com/tim-janik/jj-fzf)

**What it is:** A terminal UI for jj built on top of fzf (the fuzzy finder). It centers around the `jj log` graph view with a side preview pane showing diffs/details for the selected revision.

**DAG visualization:** Yes -- it displays the `jj log` graph directly in the fzf list pane. The DAG with parent-child lines is rendered by jj's own log formatter. You can edit the revset expression live in the fzf input field and the log reloads dynamically, so you can zoom in/out on different parts of the graph.

**Interactive navigation:** You scroll through revisions in the fzf list with fuzzy-filter capability. The preview window updates to show the selected revision's details, message, and diff. Not a free-form graph explorer -- navigation is list-based with the graph drawn as ASCII art alongside.

**Parent-child operations:** Alt-P opens a dialog to edit/simplify parents of a merge commit, enabling "mega-merge" workflows. Rebasing, squashing, and other parent-child restructuring operations are available via keybindings.

**Native jj support:** Yes, works directly with jj. No git compatibility layer needed. All commands are printed to stderr so you learn the underlying jj CLI.

**Strengths:** Lightweight (shell script + fzf), discoverable keybindings, live revset editing, operation log browsing (Ctrl-O for undo/redo history), Emacs integration available. Very actively maintained (v0.25.0+ with major features).

**Weaknesses:** The graph is essentially `jj log` ASCII output inside an fzf list -- not a purpose-built graph renderer. Navigation is linear (up/down through a list), not spatial graph traversal. Requires fzf as a dependency.

---

## 2. GG (gg-cli)

**Repository:** [github.com/gulbanana/gg](https://github.com/gulbanana/gg)

**What it is:** A desktop/web GUI for jj built with Tauri (Rust backend, Svelte frontend). Can also run as `gg web` to serve a browser-based UI.

**DAG visualization:** Yes -- the left pane shows a visual commit graph that you can query with revsets. This is a proper graphical DAG rendering, not just ASCII art.

**Interactive navigation:** Highly interactive. Click to select revisions. Double-click to edit or create a new child. The right pane shows details, descriptions, parents, and changes for the selected revision. You can drag-and-drop revisions to rebase them, drag files to squash them into different revisions, and drag bookmarks to set/delete them.

**Parent-child operations:** Excellent. You can move revisions into or out of another revision's parents to add/remove merge edges and move entire subtrees. The direct-manipulation paradigm means you are essentially always in an "interactive rebase" mode.

**Native jj support:** Yes, built specifically for jj. Install via `cargo install --locked gg-cli`.

**Strengths:** True graphical DAG with drag-and-drop manipulation, the closest thing to a visual DAG editor for jj. Runs as desktop app or web server. Supports customizable revsets. Has an "ignore immutable" toggle.

**Weaknesses:** Requires a full GUI environment (Tauri/WebView or browser). Heavier than terminal-based tools. Being a relatively niche project, community is smaller.

---

## 3. gitk

**What it is:** The classic Tcl/Tk GUI bundled with Git for visualizing commit history as a graph.

**DAG visualization:** Yes -- gitk is well-known for rendering the commit DAG with colored lines showing parent-child relationships, branch points, and merges.

**Interactive navigation:** Click on any commit to see its details, diff, author, etc. Can search commits, filter by path, and navigate the graph visually.

**jj compatibility:** Indirect. In a colocated jj repo (where `.jj` and `.git` coexist), gitk can read the underlying `.git` directory. However, jj puts Git into a **detached HEAD** state, so gitk will show that oddity. Bookmarks that jj exports as Git branches will appear, but jj-internal state (change IDs, the working-copy commit marker, revsets) are invisible to gitk. You would need to run `jj git export` (automatic in colocated repos) to ensure Git refs are up to date.

**Strengths:** Mature, well-understood, excellent graph rendering, works out of the box if you have a colocated repo.

**Weaknesses:** Shows Git's view of the repo, not jj's view -- no change IDs, no revsets, no jj-specific concepts. Detached HEAD display is confusing. Cannot perform jj operations. Feels dated. Does not work at all with non-colocated jj repos.

---

## 4. jj log (built-in)

**What it is:** jj's built-in command for displaying the commit graph in the terminal.

**DAG visualization:** Yes. Renders an ASCII/Unicode DAG with parent-child edges. The working-copy commit is marked with `@`, immutable commits with a diamond, others with a circle. Symbols are customizable.

**Interactive navigation:** Not interactive by default -- it outputs to stdout. However, `jj log` is designed to be piped or wrapped. The tool **jjiles** exists as a drop-in wrapper to make `jj log` reactive and interactive. Also, as of jj v0.39, `jj arrange` opens a built-in TUI for reordering/abandoning revisions visually.

**Revset filtering:** This is where `jj log` truly shines. The `-r` flag accepts revset expressions -- a powerful functional language for selecting commits. You can write things like `ancestors(main..@)`, `bookmarks()`, or custom named revsets. Default output is already smartly filtered to show only "interesting" commits.

**Template customization:** The `--template` flag allows full control over what information is displayed per commit.

**Strengths:** Always available (no extra install), deeply integrated with jj concepts (change IDs, revsets, operation log), extremely customizable via revsets and templates, fast.

**Weaknesses:** Not interactive (static output). ASCII graph rendering is functional but less readable than a GUI for complex merge topologies. For large repos, you need good revset discipline to avoid overwhelming output.

---

## 5. Other Notable Tools

### lazyjj
**Repository:** [github.com/Cretezy/lazyjj](https://github.com/Cretezy/lazyjj)

A Rust/Ratatui TUI inspired by lazygit. Has tabs for log, files, and bookmarks. Supports creating, editing, squashing, abandoning, describing, and bookmarking changes. Has mouse support. Works as a wrapper around the jj CLI. The DAG is shown in the log tab (rendered from `jj log` output), but DAG visualization does not appear to be its primary focus -- it is more of an operations TUI.

### jjui
**Repository:** [github.com/idursun/jjui](https://github.com/idursun/jjui)

A Go-based TUI that is gaining significant traction. Features a Revisions view with the commit graph, a Preview view for diffs, and a Details view for file-level exploration. Supports rebasing branches in the revision tree, squashing, fuzzy file finding ("Ace Jump" for fast navigation between revisions). Has a plugin system with Lua scripting and TOML-configurable keybindings. Appears to be the most popular TUI among jj users currently.

### jjdag
**Repository:** [github.com/anthrofract/jjdag](https://github.com/anthrofract/jjdag)

A Rust TUI explicitly focused on DAG manipulation, inspired by Magit. Supports browsing the jj log tree with dynamic folding/unfolding of commits and file diffs. Uses multi-key command sequences (e.g., `gpa` for `jj git push --all`, `ss` to squash). Pre-alpha status but the author uses it daily.

### jjgui
**Website:** [jj-gui.com](https://jj-gui.com/)

A closed-source native desktop GUI with a visual commit graph, AI integration (for commit messages, conflict resolution), GitHub-style code review, and multi-workspace support. Requires the `gh` CLI for GitHub features. Commercial/proprietary.

### jjiles
A `jj log` drop-in wrapper that makes the log output reactive and interactive -- essentially turning the static `jj log` into a live-updating, navigable view.

---

## Summary Comparison

| Tool | DAG Display | Interactive | Native jj | Install Weight |
|------|------------|-------------|-----------|---------------|
| **jj log** | ASCII graph, excellent revset filtering | No (static output) | Yes | Built-in |
| **jj-fzf** | jj log graph in fzf list | Yes (list-based, fuzzy filter) | Yes | Light (shell + fzf) |
| **GG (gg-cli)** | True graphical DAG, drag-and-drop | Yes (full GUI) | Yes | Medium (Tauri app) |
| **gitk** | Graphical DAG with colored lines | Yes (click-to-explore) | No (Git layer only) | Bundled with Git |
| **jjui** | Revision tree view | Yes (TUI, keybinds + mouse) | Yes | Light (Go binary) |
| **lazyjj** | Log tab with graph | Yes (TUI, tabs) | Yes | Light (Rust binary) |
| **jjdag** | Foldable log tree | Yes (TUI, Magit-style keys) | Yes | Light (Rust binary) |
| **jjgui** | Visual graph + AI | Yes (full GUI) | Yes | Heavy (closed-source desktop app) |

**For pure DAG visualization and manipulation**, GG (gg-cli) is the strongest -- it renders a true graphical DAG and lets you drag commits to restructure parent-child relationships. For terminal-only workflows, jj-fzf and jjui are the most capable, with jjui currently having the most community momentum.

> AGENT

Here's my take, informed by research into the current ecosystem:

## The short answer: gitk is a crutch, not a solution

Using gitk for a jj repo is like reading a French novel through Google Translate — you get the plot but lose the meaning. gitk only sees Git's view: no change IDs, no revsets, detached HEAD confusion, no jj-specific concepts. It works in colocated repos but it's fundamentally the wrong lens.

## My opinion on the tools

**`jj log` is underrated.** Its revset language is the real power for understanding parent-child relationships. Something like `jj log -r 'ancestors(main..@)'` or `jj log -r 'children(x)'` lets you surgically query the DAG. The ASCII rendering is basic but functional. The problem is it's static — no interactivity.

**jj-fzf** is clever but it's still `jj log` in a fuzzy-finder wrapper. The graph is there but navigation is list-based (up/down), not graph-based (follow this edge to the parent). Good for operations, not great for spatial understanding of the DAG.

**gg-cli** is the standout for what you're asking about. It renders a true graphical DAG and lets you drag-and-drop commits to restructure parent-child relationships. It's the closest thing to a visual DAG editor for jj. Runs as a desktop app (Tauri) or `gg web` in a browser. If your goal is "see and manipulate the parent-child graph," this is the tool.

**jjui** (Go TUI) is gaining the most community momentum right now — revision tree view, rebasing branches visually, plugin system with Lua scripting. Worth watching.

**lazyjj** and **jjdag** are lighter alternatives, with jjdag specifically focused on DAG manipulation (Magit-style keybindings, foldable tree), though it's still pre-alpha.

## What I'd actually recommend

1. **For quick DAG queries**: `jj log` with targeted revsets — it's always there and the revset language is expressive enough to answer most "who's the parent/child of what" questions.

2. **For visual graph exploration**: **gg-cli** (`cargo install --locked gg-cli`, then `gg` or `gg web`). It's the only tool that treats the DAG as a first-class visual, interactive, manipulable object.

3. **For daily workflow TUI**: **jjui** — it balances graph visibility with practical operations better than the alternatives.

The jj ecosystem is still young and no single tool nails "interactive DAG navigator" the way gitk did for git. But gg-cli comes closest, and it does it natively with jj concepts rather than through a git compatibility shim. That matters.

> DEVELOPER

I was looking for what gitk, in the parent and child list I can click and it immediately jumps to that commit. gg-cli can do that for parent but if the parent is not in view in the left panel it doesn't "jump" to it you literally have to "scroll" using the slider so useless on a large complex repo, IMO.

> AGENT

That's a really valid critique. If gg-cli can't jump to an out-of-view parent (let alone navigate to children), then it's just a pretty graph you have to manually scroll — which defeats the purpose on any non-trivial repo.

So gitk's killer feature here is **click-to-jump navigation** in both the parent and child lists. You see a commit, its parents and children are listed, you click one, and the view instantly repositions. That's true graph traversal, not scrolling.

And honestly... none of the jj-native tools seem to have this. They all treat the log as a list you scroll through or filter, not a graph you traverse edge-by-edge. Even jjui and jj-fzf are fundamentally list navigators with a graph drawn alongside.

My revised opinion: **gitk is still the best tool for this specific task** — interactive DAG traversal via parent/child links — even with its jj blindspots. The tradeoff (losing change IDs, seeing detached HEAD weirdness) is worth it when the alternative is manually scrolling a graph.

What the jj ecosystem is missing is a tool that combines:
- jj-native concepts (change IDs, revsets, bookmarks)
- gitk-style click-to-jump on parent/child links
- works on large repos without degrading to scroll-based navigation

That's a gap worth filing as a feature request on gg-cli — the infrastructure is there (it already shows parents in the detail pane), it just needs the jump-to behavior wired up.

   115→
   116→## Use commit titles to cross-reference changeIDs
   117→
   118→This was tried but I decided footers were more flexible and less noice than the commit title.
   119→See [ChangeID footer syntax](#changeid-footer-syntax) for the final approach.
   120→
   121→A **critical item** in this experiment is each commit can contain the changeID
   122→of the other commit. Here I'm trying out providing both "this commit id" (tcid)
   123→and "other commit id" (ocid) in the commit title to make it easier for tools to
   124→navigate and synchronize the two repos.
   125→
   126→title syntax: `<tcid>-<ocid>-<short description>`
   127→
   128→ - `<tcid>` is the jj change ID of this commit and is never `none`.
   129→ - `<ocid>` is the jj change ID of the other commit and may be `none` if the bot was not involved.
   130→ - `<short description>` is a brief one line description of the chore.
   131→
   132→The intent is that these might be able to make the tool that shows the
   133→evolution of the code more clear by showing the relationship between the two repos
   134→
   135→## zznknssm-none-Validate changeID consistency
   136→
   137→Validate that when we clone a vc repo that the changeID remains the same
   138→between the original and the clone for both the main and the claude repos.
   139→
   140→Move the original local repo to vc-x1.ok and
   141→clone to vc-x1 and then Initialize jj in vc-x1

   221→
   222→## Use footers to track changeIDs or notes
   223→
   224→> **Note:** The original approach used markdown reference-link syntax
   225→> in commit footers. We now use
   226→> [git trailers](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-interpret-trailers)
   227→> (`key: value` format) instead, as they are a standard convention
   228→> and parseable by `git interpret-trailers` and other tools.
   229→
   230→Since jj changeIDs are generated at `jj git init` time (not stored in the
   231→repo), changeID references are only resolvable by tools that have access
   232→to the local jj repo (e.g. vc-x1).
   233→
   234→## Git trailer convention
   235→
   236→We use [git trailers](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-interpret-trailers)
   237→in commit messages for inter-repo cross-references. Trailers appear as
   238→blank-line-separated `key: value` lines at the end of the commit body.
   239→
   240→### ochid (Other Change ID)
   241→
   242→The `ochid` trailer links a commit to its counterpart in another repo
   243→within the workspace. The value is a workspace-root-relative path
   244→followed by the jj changeID:
   245→
   246→- `ochid: /changeID` — references the workspace-root repo (vc-x1)
   247→- `ochid: /.claude/changeID` — references the .claude sub-repo
   248→
   249→Example commit message:
   250→```
   251→Add jj tips for git users to README
   252→
   253→Add jj tips section to README explaining why jj log
   254→shows fewer commits than gitk.
   255→
   256→ochid: /.claude/xvzvruqo
   257→```
   258→
   259→### ChangeID path syntax
   260→
   261→All changeID paths are **workspace-root relative** (start with `/`):
   262→
   263→- `/` is the workspace root (the app repo, vc-x1)
   264→- `/.claude` is the bot session sub-repo
   265→
   266→This means `ochid: /wtpmottv` refers to a change in the app repo,
   267→**not** the .claude repo. The leading `/` anchors to the workspace
   268→root, not the current repo.
   269→
   270→### .vc-config.toml
   271→
   272→Each repo contains a `.vc-config.toml` that identifies its location
   273→within the workspace. This avoids repeating the workspace-path in
   274→every commit trailer.
   275→
   276→```toml
   277→# In vc-x1 (workspace root):
   278→[workspace]
   279→path = "/"
   280→
   281→# In .claude (sub-repo):
   282→[workspace]
   283→path = "/.claude"
   284→```
   285→
   286→Tools can read `.vc-config.toml` to resolve changeID paths in
   287→`ochid` trailers to the correct local repo.
   288→
   289→## Create a binary that lists jj info
   290→
   291→This binary should list the changeID, commitID, and description title
   292→and using `jj-lib`
   293→
   294→### Implementation
   295→
   296→Created a Rust binary (`src/main.rs`) using `jj-lib` 0.39.0 that:
   297→
   298→1. Opens the jj workspace from the current directory (or a path argument)
   299→2. Loads the repo at head via `Workspace::load()` and `RepoLoader::load_at_head()`
   300→3. Evaluates `RevsetExpression::all()` to iterate all commits
   301→4. For each commit (excluding the root), prints: changeID (reverse hex, 12 chars),
   302→   commitID (hex, 12 chars), and first line of description
   303→
   304→Key dependencies: `jj-lib = "0.39.0"`, `pollster = "0.4"` (for async `.block_on()`).
   305→
   306→Usage:
   307→```
   308→cargo run            # uses current directory
   309→cargo run -- /path   # uses specified path
   310→```
   311→
   312→## Finalize subcommand for session repo coherence
   313→
   314→### Problem
   315→
   316→When the bot commits and pushes the `.claude` session repo as its last action,
   317→the act of committing itself generates more `.jsonl` session data. This means
   318→the commit is always missing the trailing writes — information from commit N
   319→shows up in commit N+1.
   320→
   321→### Discovery
   322→
   323→We tested a `nohup bash -c "sleep N; jj squash; ..." &` pattern to delay the
   324→squash until after trailing writes settle. Findings:
   325→
   326→- **0.5s** is the minimum reliable delay; 0.25s also worked, 0.1s did not
   327→- **Shell `&` operator** triggers Claude Code's safety prompt ("shell operators
   328→  that require approval") — cannot be auto-approved via permission rules
   329→- **`run_in_background`** (Claude Code's built-in) doesn't help because it
   330→  blocks the bash slot and waits, so the session finishes writing before the
   331→  sleep even starts — no trailing writes to capture
   332→
   333→### Solution: `vc-x1 finalize` subcommand
   334→
   335→A Rust binary can daemonize itself via `std::process::Command` (detached spawn)
   336→without needing shell `&`. The permission rule `Bash(vc-x1 finalize *)` works
   337→cleanly.
   338→
   339→**Usage:**
   340→```
   341→vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --delay 1 --push
   342→```
   343→
   344→**Options:**
   345→- `--repo <path>` — path to jj repo (default: `.`)
   346→- `--source <revset>` — revision to squash (default: `@`)
   347→- `--target <revset>` — revision to squash into (default: `@-`)
   348→- `--delay <seconds>` — seconds to wait before squashing (default: `1`)
   349→- `--push` — push after squashing
   350→- `--log <path>` — log file (default: `/tmp/vc-x1-finalize-<timestamp-millis>.log`)
   351→- `--foreground` — skip daemonization, run in foreground
   352→
   353→### Implementation plan
   354→
   355→- **0.6.0-dev1** (done): arg parsing for finalize subcommand
   356→- **0.6.0-dev2** (done): daemonize — binary re-spawns itself detached with
   357→  internal `--exec` flag, parent returns immediately. Added `log_msg` helper
   358→  for nanosecond-timestamped append-mode logging throughout the flow.
   359→- **0.6.0-dev3** (done): implement finalize logic — daemonized child sleeps,
   360→  then shells out to `jj squash`, `jj bookmark set`, `jj git push`
   361→- **0.6.0-dev4** (done): add `--ignore-immutable` to squash, unique log paths
   362→  per invocation using timestamp-millis
   363→- **0.6.0**: finalize release — remove `-devN`, update docs/todo/chores
   364→
   365→### BREAKING-CHANGE trailer
   366→
   367→During this work we confirmed that `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with space) is the only
   368→space-separated git trailer key allowed per the Conventional Commits spec. We
   369→adopted the hyphenated form `BREAKING-CHANGE:` as it's also valid and avoids
   370→the space ambiguity.
   371→
   372→## Refactor and add `desc` subcommand
   373→
   374→### Motivation
   375→
   376→`main.rs` has grown to ~585 lines with list, finalize, and all parsing in one
   377→file. Before adding more subcommands we should split into modules. The first
   378→new subcommand, `desc`, displays a commit's full description by changeID —
   379→a building block toward a jj-lib interface layer.
   380→
   381→### `desc` subcommand
   382→
   383→```
   384→Usage: vc-x1 desc <chid> [OPTIONS]
   385→
   386→Display the full description of a commit identified by its changeID.
   387→
   388→Arguments:
   389→  <chid>            The jj changeID (or prefix) to look up
   390→
   391→Options:
   392→  --repo <path>     Path to jj repo (default: current directory)
   393→  -h, --help        Print this help message
   394→```
   395→
   396→Uses jj-lib to resolve the changeID via revset, then prints the commit's full
   397→description text.
   398→
   399→### File layout after refactor
   400→
   401→```
   402→src/
   403→  main.rs       — USAGE, Command enum, parse_args(), main()
   404→  common.rs     — shared jj-lib helpers (load workspace/repo, resolve revset)
   405→  list.rs       — list() and its tests
   406→  finalize.rs   — FinalizeOpts, parsing, finalize/daemonize/exec, tests
   407→  desc.rs       — desc() and its tests
   408→```
   409→
   410→`common.rs` will start small (workspace+repo loading) and grow into a jj
   411→interface layer as we add more subcommands.
   412→
   413→### Implementation plan
   414→
   415→- **0.7.0-dev1**: extract `common.rs` and refactor `list` into `src/list.rs`
   416→- **0.7.0-dev2**: refactor `finalize` into `src/finalize.rs`
   417→- **0.7.0-dev3**: implement `desc` in `src/desc.rs`
   418→- **0.7.0**: final release — remove `-devN`, update todo/chores
   419→
```

     1→# Notes
     2→
     3→This directory contains various notes and documentation related to the project.
     4→Each file is organized by topic for easy reference.
     5→
     6→By default there are chores-*.md and todo.md. Chores are general notes

    16→General maintenance tasks and considerations for the project see other files for

    18→how and why of a particular chore.

    25→
    26→## Versioning during development
    27→
    28→This is using jujustiu, jj + git and we'll see how it goes. Below is my
    29→git workflow, jj will be different but we'll have to discover that as
    30→we go.
    31→
    32→Every plan must start with a version bump. Choose the approach based on scope:
    33→
    34→- **Single-step** (recommended for mechanical/focused changes): bump directly to
    35→  `X.Y.Z`, implement in one commit. Simpler history.
    36→- **Multi-step** (for exploratory/large changes): bump to `X.Y.Z-devN`, implement
    37→  across multiple commits, final commit removes `-devN`.
    38→
    39→The plan should recommend one approach and get user approval before starting.
    40→
    41→For multi-step:
    42→1. Bump version to `X.Y.Z-devN` with a plan and commit as a chore marker
    43→2. Implement in one or more `-devN` commits (bump N as needed)
    44→3. Final commit removes `-devN`, updates todo/chores — this is the "done" marker
    45→
    46→The final release commit (without `-devN`) signals completion rather than amending
    47→prior commits. This keeps the git history readable and makes it easy to see which
    48→commits were exploratory vs final.
    49→
    50→## Todo format
    51→
    52→Todo.md contains two main sections "Todo" and "Done" each item is a
    53→short explanations of a tasks and links to more details using 1 or more
    54→references.
    55→
    56→Multiple references must be separated: `[2],[3]` not `[2,3]` or `[2][3]`.
    57→In markdown, `[2,3]` is a single ref key (won't resolve) and `[2][3]`
    58→is parsed as display text `2` with ref key `3` (so `[2]` won't resolve).
    59→
    60→Examples:
    61→
    62→# Todo
    63→- Add new feature X [details](features.md#feature-x)
    64→- Fix bug Y [1]
    65→
    66→# Done
    67→- Fixed issue Z [2],[3]
    68→
    69→[1]: bugs.md#bug-y
    70→[2]: issues.md#issue-z
    71→[3]: fixes.md#fix-z
    72→
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1/notes/chores-02.md",
  "content": "# Chores-02\n\n## jj repo visualization: navigating parent-child relationships\n\n### The problem\n\nWhen working with jj-git repos, especially complex ones, you need to\nvisually navigate the commit DAG — click a parent or child and jump\nto it instantly. This is table-stakes in gitk but surprisingly absent\nfrom jj-native tooling.\n\n### Tool survey (2026-03-18)\n\n#### gitk (still the best for DAG traversal)\n\ngitk's killer feature: the parent/child list lets you **click-to-jump**\nto any commit, even if it's far off-screen. True graph traversal, not\nscrolling. Works in colocated jj repos (where `.jj` and `.git` coexist)\nvia `jj git export`.\n\n**Downsides:** Shows Git's view only — no change IDs, no revsets, no\njj bookmarks. Detached HEAD display is confusing. Cannot perform jj\noperations. Feels dated. Does not work with non-colocated jj repos.\n\n#### gg-cli (GUI for jj, Tauri/Svelte)\n\nRenders a true graphical DAG. Can show parents in the detail pane and\nclick to select a parent — but **only if that parent is already visible\nin the left panel**. If the parent is off-screen, there is no jump-to\nbehavior; you must manually scroll using the slider. This makes it\n**useless on large/complex repos** for DAG traversal.\n\nDrag-and-drop rebase and other operations are nice, but the lack of\nclick-to-jump is a dealbreaker for navigation.\n\nInstall: `cargo install --locked gg-cli`, run `gg` or `gg web`.\n\n#### jj-fzf (fzf-based TUI)\n\nDisplays `jj log` graph in an fzf list with live revset editing.\nNavigation is list-based (up/down), not graph-based (follow edges).\nGood for operations and filtering, not for spatial DAG understanding.\n\n#### jjui (Go TUI, most community momentum)\n\nRevision tree view, rebasing, plugin system (Lua scripting). Growing\nfast but still fundamentally a list navigator with a graph drawn alongside.\n\n#### jj log (built-in)\n\nRevset language is powerful for querying parent-child relationships\n(`children(x)`, `ancestors(main..@)`). But output is static — no\ninteractivity.\n\n#### Others\n\n- **lazyjj** — Rust TUI inspired by lazygit. Operations-focused, not DAG-focused.\n- **jjdag** — Pre-alpha Rust TUI explicitly for DAG manipulation (Magit-style keys, foldable tree). Worth watching.\n- **jjgui** — Closed-source native desktop GUI with AI integration. Commercial.\n\n### Summary\n\n| Tool     | DAG display | Click-to-jump parent/child | Native jj |\n|----------|-------------|---------------------------|-----------|\n| gitk     | graphical   | **yes** (both directions)  | no        |\n| gg-cli   | graphical   | parent only, no jump if off-screen | yes |\n| jj-fzf   | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |\n| jjui     | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |\n| jj log   | ASCII       | n/a (not interactive)      | yes       |\n\n### Conclusion\n\n**gitk remains the best tool for interactive DAG traversal** despite its\njj blindspots. The jj ecosystem is missing a tool that combines jj-native\nconcepts (change IDs, revsets, bookmarks) with gitk-style click-to-jump\non parent/child links. This would be a strong feature request for gg-cli,\nwhich already has the infrastructure (parent display in detail pane) but\nlacks the jump-to behavior.\n\nFor day-to-day jj work, `jj log -r <revset>` is the practical fallback\nfor understanding parent-child structure without a GUI.\n"
}

How do I recover it without changing anything else, it op a64a4 from 14 hours ago

> AGENT

You can restore it from that operation using `jj restore`:

```
jj restore --from @- --to @ notes/chores-02.md -R . --at-op a65a4
```

Actually, the simpler approach — since you just want the file content back without rewinding anything else:

```
jj cat -r @- notes/chores-02.md -R . --at-op a65a4 > notes/chores-02.md
```

Wait — `jj cat` doesn't exist. The command is `jj file show`:

```
jj file show notes/chores-02.md -r @- -R . --at-op a65a4
```

That will print the file contents. Redirect to recover it:

```
jj file show notes/chores-02.md -r @- -R . --at-op a65a4 > notes/chores-02.md
```

This recovers just that file without touching anything else in your working copy.

> DEVELOPER

In actual fact if I just did something like "git reset --hard main" I'd recover it, how do I do that in jj

> AGENT

In jj, the working copy (`@`) always tracks changes automatically. Since the file was in `@-` (the parent commit on `main`), you can just restore it from there:

```
jj restore --from @- notes/chores-02.md -R .
```

This is the jj equivalent of recovering a file from the parent commit. No need to reference a specific operation — the file is in the committed history at `@-`.

If you wanted the broader equivalent of `git reset --hard main` (discard all working copy changes), that would be:

```
jj restore -R .
```

Which restores the entire working copy to match `@-` (its parent).

     1→# Chores-02
     2→
     3→## Graphical tools for jj repos
     4→
     5→### GG (gg)
     6→
     7→Cross-platform GUI for jj (Tauri-based). Interactive graph view.
     8→
     9→- Install: https://github.com/gulbanana/gg
    10→- `gg gui` does NOT work; use `gg web` instead (opens in browser)
    11→
    12→### jj-fzf
    13→
    14→fzf-based TUI centered on `jj log` graph. Previews diffs, evolog,
    15→op log. Many keybindings for common jj operations.
    16→
    17→- Install: https://github.com/tim-janik/jj-fzf
    18→- Requires `fzf` installed separately (`sudo pacman -S fzf` on Arch)
    19→
    20→### Others (not tested)
    21→
    22→- **jjui** — TUI for jj (https://github.com/idursun/jjui)
    23→- **jjgui** — commercial desktop app (https://jj-gui.com/)
    24→- **Jujutsu Kaizen** — VS Code plugin
    25→- **VisualJJ** — VS Code plugin (native jj, no git colocation)
    26→- **Selvejj** — JetBrains plugin
    27→
    28→### Built-in
    29→
    30→- `jj log` — shows graph by default (no `--graph` flag needed)
    31→- `gitk --all` — works on the colocated `.git` but sees raw git refs,
    32→  not jj's view of history
    33→
    34→## jj commit organization and traversal mechanisms
    35→
    36→### Motivation
    37→
    38→Understanding how jj commits are organized and what jj-lib APIs exist
    39→for traversing them — a prerequisite for building richer views of
    40→codebase evolution over time. The todo item "determine the organization
    41→of commits in jj and how we can iterate over them" prompted this
    42→investigation.
    43→
    44→### Three axes of traversal
    45→
    46→jj provides three distinct ways to navigate commit history:
    47→
    48→1. **Commit DAG** — parent/child relationships between commits
    49→2. **Refs/bookmarks** — named pointers (bookmarks, tags, git refs) into the DAG
    50→3. **Evolution log (evolog)** — rewrite history of a single ChangeId across operations
    51→
    52→Each answers a different question:
    53→- DAG: "what is the commit history?"
    54→- Refs: "where are the named branches pointing?"
    55→- Evolog: "how was this particular change built up?"
    56→
    57→### Commit DAG traversal via revsets
    58→
    59→jj-lib's revset engine supports both string-based and programmatic queries.
    60→
    61→**String-based** (what vc-x1 uses today via `common::resolve_revset`):
    62→```rust
    63→resolve_revset(&workspace, &repo, "::@")?  // ancestors of @
    64→resolve_revset(&workspace, &repo, "all()")? // all visible commits
    65→```
    66→
    67→**Programmatic** (builder API, no parsing needed):
    68→```rust
    69→use jj_lib::revset::RevsetExpression;
    70→
    71→RevsetExpression::all()                    // all visible + referenced
    72→RevsetExpression::visible_heads()          // tips of visible branches
    73→RevsetExpression::symbol("@".into())       // working copy
    74→RevsetExpression::commits(vec![id])        // explicit set
    75→RevsetExpression::bookmarks(pattern)       // bookmark refs
    76→RevsetExpression::git_refs()               // all git refs
    77→
    78→// Chainable traversal
    79→expr.ancestors()           // ::x
    80→expr.descendants()         // x::
    81→expr.parents()             // direct parents
    82→expr.children()            // direct children
    83→expr.heads()               // maximal elements
    84→expr.roots()               // minimal elements
    85→expr.fork_point()          // merge base
    86→
    87→// Ranges and set operations
    88→expr1.range(&expr2)        // x..y
    89→expr1.dag_range_to(&expr2) // x::y
    90→expr1.union(&expr2)
    91→expr1.intersection(&expr2)
    92→expr1.minus(&expr2)
    93→```
    94→
    95→### Iteration modes
    96→
    97→Evaluated revsets support three iteration modes:
    98→
    99→```rust
   100→let revset = expr.evaluate(repo)?;
   101→
   102→revset.iter()               // yields CommitId in topo order (newest first)
   103→revset.commit_change_ids()  // yields (CommitId, ChangeId) pairs
   104→revset.iter_graph()         // yields GraphNode<CommitId> with edge info
   105→```
   106→
   107→`iter_graph()` provides `GraphEdge` data with edge types (`Direct`,
   108→`Indirect`, `Missing`) — needed for rendering graph views like
   109→`jj log --graph`.
   110→
   111→### Ref/bookmark access via View
   112→
   113→```rust
   114→let view = repo.view();
   115→view.heads()              // HashSet<CommitId> — visible branch tips
   116→view.bookmarks()          // all bookmarks with local+remote state
   117→view.local_bookmarks()    // local bookmarks only
   118→view.git_refs()           // BTreeMap of git refs
   119→view.git_head()           // git HEAD
   120→```
   121→
   122→### Index (direct DAG queries)
   123→
   124→```rust
   125→let index = repo.index();
   126→index.is_ancestor(&id1, &id2)?           // ancestry check
   127→index.common_ancestors(&[id1], &[id2])?  // merge base
   128→index.heads(&mut candidates)?            // compute heads
   129→index.all_heads_for_gc()?                // every head including hidden
   130→```
   131→
   132→### Low-level DAG walks (dag_walk module)
   133→
   134→Generic traversal functions on arbitrary DAGs:
   135→
   136→```rust
   137→jj_lib::dag_walk::dfs(start, id_fn, neighbors_fn)
   138→jj_lib::dag_walk::topo_order_forward(...)
   139→jj_lib::dag_walk::topo_order_reverse(...)
   140→jj_lib::dag_walk::heads(...)
   141→jj_lib::dag_walk::closest_common_node(...)
   142→```
   143→
   144→### Evolution log (evolog)
   145→
   146→The evolog shows every version a ChangeId went through — snapshots,
   147→squashes, rebases, describes. It's tracked at the **operation level**,
   148→not in the commit DAG.
   149→
   150→Example output from `jj evolog -r @-`:
   151→```
   152→◆    rpyrwwnw wink@saville.com 2026-03-17 09:53:12 main f7f567f6
   153→├─╮  Finalize: replace --foreground with --detach, ...
   154→│ │  -- operation 655ff50a3386 squash commits into ...
   155→│ ○  tluyvppo/0 wink@saville.com 2026-03-17 09:53:12 89b1c104 (hidden)
   156→│ │  (no description set)
   157→│ │  -- operation e71fa874e37a snapshot working copy
   158→│ ○  tluyvppo/1 wink@saville.com 2026-03-17 09:49:54 317cefcf (hidden)
   159→│    (empty) (no description set)
   160→│    -- operation e1fe385617e5 commit ...
   161→○  rpyrwwnw/1 wink@saville.com 2026-03-17 09:49:54 50c30496 (hidden)
   162→│  Finalize: replace --foreground with --detach, ...
   163→...
   164→```
   165→
   166→**Core API** (`jj_lib::evolution`):
   167→
   168→```rust
   169→use jj_lib::evolution::{walk_predecessors, CommitEvolutionEntry};
   170→
   171→// Walk the rewrite history of a commit
   172→let iter = walk_predecessors(repo, &[commit_id]);
   173→for entry in iter {
   174→    let entry: CommitEvolutionEntry = entry?;
   175→    // entry.commit        — the commit at this point in history
   176→    // entry.operation      — the operation that created this version
   177→    // entry.predecessor_ids() — IDs of the previous version(s)
   178→    // entry.predecessors()    — iterator over predecessor Commit objects
   179→}
   180→
   181→// Batch resolve predecessors across operation ranges
   182→let map: BTreeMap<CommitId, Vec<CommitId>> =
   183→    accumulate_predecessors(new_ops, old_ops)?;
   184→```
   185→
   186→### Evolog data is local-only
   187→
   188→**Critical finding**: operation/evolution data does NOT survive a git
   189→push + clone cycle. This is by design.
   190→
   191→From `op_store.rs`:
   192→> "Operations and views are not meant to be exchanged between repos or
   193→> users; they represent local state and history."
   194→
   195→| Data                  | Pushed? | Recoverable on clone? |
   196→|-----------------------|---------|----------------------|
   197→| Git commits + trees   | Yes     | Yes                  |
   198→| Change IDs            | No      | Yes (from git header)|
   199→| Commit predecessors   | No      | No                   |
   200→| Operation history     | No      | No                   |
   201→| Evolution log         | No      | No                   |
   202→
   203→Predecessor tracking is stored per-operation in
   204→`op_store::Operation::commit_predecessors` (since jj 0.30). The old
   205→`backend::Commit::predecessors` field is deprecated and slated for
   206→removal around jj 0.42.
   207→
   208→### Implications for codebase evolution story
   209→
   210→The evolog provides a rich local narrative of how changes were built
   211→up — intermediate snapshots, false starts, squashes — that commit
   212→messages alone don't capture. However, this data is ephemeral across
   213→git transport.
   214→
   215→For the dual-repo workflow (app + `.claude`), the `.claude` repo's
   216→evolog captures how session commits were constructed. If `.claude`
   217→eventually becomes a git submodule or is backed by a database, the
   218→evolog data could be extracted and persisted via `walk_predecessors()`
   219→before it's lost to a clone. This would preserve the full provenance
   220→chain: not just what changed, but how the change was assembled.
   221→
   222→### Key jj-lib source files (0.39.0)
   223→
   224→- `src/revset.rs` — revset expression types, builders, parsing
   225→- `src/evolution.rs` — evolog/predecessor traversal
   226→- `src/graph.rs` — graph structures, TopoGroupedGraphIterator
   227→- `src/dag_walk.rs` — generic DAG traversal (DFS, topo sort)
   228→- `src/repo.rs` — Repo trait, ReadonlyRepo
   229→- `src/view.rs` — View (heads, bookmarks, git refs)
   230→- `src/index.rs` — Index trait (ancestry, common ancestors)
   231→- `src/commit.rs` — Commit wrapper (parent_ids, change_id, description)
   232→- `src/backend.rs` — backend data types (CommitId, ChangeId, Signature)
   233→- `src/op_store.rs` — operation storage (commit_predecessors)
   234→- `src/operation.rs` — Operation type (predecessors_for_commit)
   235→
```

This is table-stakes in gitk but absent from\njj-native tooling.\n\n### gitk: still the best for DAG traversal\n\ngitk's killer feature: the parent/child list lets you **click-to-jump**\nto any commit, even if it's far off-screen. True graph traversal, not\nscrolling. Works in colocated jj repos via `jj git export`.\n\n**Downsides:** Shows Git's view only — no change IDs, no revsets, no\njj bookmarks. Detached HEAD display is confusing. Cannot perform jj\noperations. Does not work with non-colocated jj repos.\n\n### gg-cli: close but not there\n\nRenders a true graphical DAG. Can show parents in the detail pane and\nclick to select a parent — but **only if that parent is already visible\nin the left panel**. If the parent is off-screen, there is no jump-to\nbehavior; you must manually scroll using the slider. This makes it\n**useless on large/complex repos** for DAG traversal.\n\nDrag-and-drop rebase is nice, but the lack of click-to-jump is a\ndealbreaker for navigation.\n\n### Other tools: list navigators, not graph navigators\n\n- **jj-fzf** — displays `jj log` graph in fzf with live revset editing.\n  Navigation is list-based (up/down), not graph-based (follow edges).\n- **jjui** — revision tree view, growing fast, but still fundamentally\n  a list navigator with a graph drawn alongside.\n- **lazyjj** — operations-focused TUI, not DAG-focused.\n- **jjdag** — pre-alpha, explicitly DAG-focused (Magit-style keys,\n  foldable tree). Worth watching.\n- **jj log** — revset language is powerful for querying (`children(x)`,\n  `ancestors(main..@)`) but output is static, not interactive.\n\n### Comparison\n\n| Tool     | DAG display | Click-to-jump parent/child | Native jj |\n|----------|-------------|---------------------------|-----------|\n| gitk     | graphical   | **yes** (both directions)  | no        |\n| gg-cli   | graphical   | parent only, no jump if off-screen | yes |\n| jj-fzf   | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |\n| jjui     | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |\n| jj log   | ASCII       | n/a (not interactive)      | yes       |\n\n### Conclusion\n\n**gitk remains the best tool for interactive DAG traversal** despite\nits jj blindspots. The jj ecosystem is missing a tool that combines\njj-native concepts (change IDs, revsets, bookmarks) with gitk-style\nclick-to-jump on parent/child links. This would be a strong feature\nrequest for gg-cli, which already has the infrastructure but lacks the\njump-to behavior.\n\n### Cautionary tale: multi-tool dangers\n\nWhile testing jj-fzf and gg-cli simultaneously (with Zed editor also\nopen), it's easy to accidentally move `@` to an unexpected commit via\none tool without noticing. When Zed then sees files disappear from the\nworking copy, closing a \"changed\" file and declining to save effectively\ndeletes it. Recovery: `jj new main` to repoint `@`, or\n`jj file show <path> -r <rev> --at-op <op>` to extract from history.\n\n### Key jj-lib source files (0.39.0)"

Perfect! Now I have all the information needed. Let me create a comprehensive summary of the jj-lib Commit type API:

## Comprehensive jj-lib 0.39.0 Commit Type API Reference

Based on my analysis of the jj-lib source code at `/home/wink/.cargo/registry/src/index.crates.io-1949cf8c6b5b557f/jj-lib-0.39.0/src/`, here's the complete jj-lib Commit type API:

### File Location
`/home/wink/.cargo/registry/src/index.crates.io-1949cf8c6b5b557f/jj-lib-0.39.0/src/commit.rs`

### Commit Type Definition

```rust
pub struct Commit {
    store: Arc<Store>,
    id: CommitId,
    data: Arc<backend::Commit>,
}
```

### Full Public API for `impl Commit`

#### 1. **Constructor & Access to Store**

- `pub fn new(store: Arc<Store>, id: CommitId, data: Arc<backend::Commit>) -> Self` — Creates a new Commit instance
- `pub fn store(&self) -> &Arc<Store>` — Returns reference to the commit's Store
- `pub fn store_commit(&self) -> &Arc<backend::Commit>` — Returns the underlying backend Commit data

#### 2. **Commit Identification**

- `pub fn id(&self) -> &CommitId` — Returns the CommitId (commit hash)
- `pub fn change_id(&self) -> &ChangeId` — Returns the ChangeId (tracks logical changes across commits)

#### 3. **Parent Access**

- `pub fn parent_ids(&self) -> &[CommitId]` — Returns slice of parent CommitIds (synchronous, just IDs)
- `pub async fn parents(&self) -> BackendResult<Vec<Self>>` — Asynchronously loads full parent Commit objects

#### 4. **Tree/Snapshot Access** (Core API for getting file state)

- `pub fn tree(&self) -> MergedTree` — Returns the MergedTree representing the commit's file snapshot/state
- `pub fn tree_ids(&self) -> &Merge<TreeId>` — Returns the underlying tree IDs (may be conflicted, returns `Merge<TreeId>`)
- `pub async fn parent_tree(&self, repo: &dyn Repo) -> BackendResult<MergedTree>` — Returns merged tree of all parents (handles merge commits). If single parent, returns that parent's tree. If multiple parents, merges them. Used as the baseline for computing commit's changes.

#### 5. **Description & Metadata**

- `pub fn description(&self) -> &str` — Returns the commit message
- `pub fn author(&self) -> &Signature` — Returns author signature (name, email, timestamp)
- `pub fn committer(&self) -> &Signature` — Returns committer signature (name, email, timestamp)

#### 6. **Commit State Queries**

- `pub fn has_conflict(&self) -> bool` — Returns true if the tree has merge conflicts
- `pub fn is_empty(&self, repo: &dyn Repo) -> BackendResult<bool>` — Returns true if commit has no changes (content is identical to parent tree)
- `pub fn is_discardable(&self, repo: &dyn Repo) -> BackendResult<bool>` — Returns true if commit is both empty AND has no description
- `pub fn is_hidden(&self, repo: &dyn Repo) -> IndexResult<bool>` — Returns true if commit is hidden from the change ID index
- `pub fn is_signed(&self) -> bool` — Quick check if commit has a signature present

#### 7. **Verification**

- `pub fn verification(&self) -> SignResult<Option<Verification>>` — Gets full signature verification status (slower, cached)

#### 8. **Conflict Markers**

- `pub fn conflict_label(&self) -> String` — Returns a label for conflict markers (includes change ID, commit ID, first line of description)
- `pub async fn parents_conflict_label(&self) -> BackendResult<String>` — Returns conflict label for the parent commits

### How to Get Diffs/Changes for a Commit

**There is no single method on Commit to get diffs directly.** Instead, use:

1. **Get the parent tree** using `commit.parent_tree(repo).await`
2. **Get the commit tree** using `commit.tree()`
3. **Compare trees** using `MergedTree::diff_stream()`:

```rust
let commit_tree = commit.tree();
let parent_tree = commit.parent_tree(repo).await?;

// Create a matcher (EverythingMatcher for all files)
use jj_lib::matchers::EverythingMatcher;
let matcher = EverythingMatcher;

// Get diff entries as async stream
let diff_stream = parent_tree.diff_stream(&commit_tree, &matcher);

// Iterate through changes
while let Some(entry) = diff_stream.next().await {
    let path = entry.path; // RepoPathBuf
    let values = entry.values?; // Diff<MergedTreeValue>
}
```

### How to Get Children of a Commit

**The Commit type does NOT have a method to get children.** Children must be found via:

1. **Using revsets** through `repo.index()`:
```rust
let revset_str = format!("children({})", commit.id().hex());
// Parse and evaluate revset to get child commits
```

2. **Or using index queries** (if available in your version)

The `Index` trait (accessed via `repo.index()`) provides:
- `is_ancestor()` — Check if a commit is an ancestor of another
- `evaluate_revset()` — Evaluate revset expressions including `children()`
- `changed_paths_in_commit()` — Get paths changed by a specific commit

### MergedTree Type (For Working with File Trees)

Location: `/home/wink/.cargo/registry/src/index.crates.io-1949cf8c6b5b557f/jj-lib-0.39.0/src/merged_tree.rs`

Key methods for diffing:

```rust
pub struct MergedTree { ... }

// Accessing tree data
pub fn tree_ids(&self) -> &Merge<TreeId>
pub fn store(&self) -> &Arc<Store>
pub async fn trees(&self) -> BackendResult<Merge<Tree>>

// Diffing
pub fn diff_stream<'matcher>(
    &self,
    other: &Self,
    matcher: &'matcher dyn Matcher,
) -> TreeDiffStream<'matcher>

pub fn diff_stream_with_trees<'matcher>(
    &self,
    other: &Self,
    matcher: &'matcher dyn Matcher,
) -> TreeDiffStream<'matcher>

pub fn diff_stream_for_file_system<'matcher>(
    &self,
    other: &Self,
    matcher: &'matcher dyn Matcher,
) -> TreeDiffStream<'matcher>

pub fn diff_stream_with_copies<'a>(
    &self,
    other: &Self,
    matcher: &'a dyn Matcher,
    copy_records: &'a CopyRecords,
) -> BoxStream<'a, CopiesTreeDiffEntry>

// Checking for conflicts
pub fn conflicts(&self) -> impl Iterator<Item = (RepoPathBuf, BackendResult<MergedTreeValue>)>
pub fn has_conflict(&self) -> bool

// Accessing specific paths
pub fn path_value(&self, path: &RepoPath) -> BackendResult<MergedTreeValue>
pub async fn path_value_async(&self, path: &RepoPath) -> BackendResult<MergedTreeValue>

// Entry iteration
pub fn entries(&self) -> TreeEntriesIterator<'static>
pub fn entries_matching<'matcher>(
    &self,
    matcher: &'matcher dyn Matcher,
) -> TreeEntriesIterator<'matcher>
```

}

### Practical Example: Getting Commit Changes

```rust
use jj_lib::matchers::EverythingMatcher;
use futures::StreamExt;

async fn get_commit_changes(commit: &Commit, repo: &dyn Repo) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let commit_tree = commit.tree();
    let parent_tree = commit.parent_tree(repo).await?;
    
    let matcher = EverythingMatcher;
    let mut diff_stream = parent_tree.diff_stream(&commit_tree, &matcher);
    
    while let Some(entry) = diff_stream.next().await {
        println!("Path: {}", entry.path);
        match entry.values {
            Ok(diff) => {
                println!("  Before: {:?}", diff.before);
                println!("  After: {:?}", diff.after);
            }
            Err(e) => println!("  Error: {}", e),
        }
    }
    Ok(())
}
```

|-------|--------|---------|-------|
| Get commit ID | `id()` | `&CommitId` | Synchronous |
| Get change ID | `change_id()` | `&ChangeId` | For tracking changes |
| Get parents | `parents()` | `BackendResult<Vec<Commit>>` | Async |
| Get parent IDs | `parent_ids()` | `&[CommitId]` | Synchronous, just IDs |
| Get file state | `tree()` | `MergedTree` | The snapshot of files at this commit |
| Get tree IDs | `tree_ids()` | `&Merge<TreeId>` | May be conflicted |
| Get parent tree | `parent_tree(repo)` | `BackendResult<MergedTree>` | Async, merged parent tree |
| Get diff to parent | Use `diff_stream()` on trees | `TreeDiffStream` | Compare `parent_tree()` with `tree()` |
| Get children | Revset `children(id)` | Requires `repo.index()` | NOT on Commit type |
| Check if empty | `is_empty(repo)` | `BackendResult<bool>` | No changes from parent |
| Check conflicts | `has_conflict()` | `bool` | Tree has merge conflicts |

> AGENT

You're right on both counts:

**Children** — not on `Commit`. Parent→child is a reverse lookup. You need revsets:
```rust
resolve_revset(&workspace, &repo, &format!("children({})", change_id))?
```

**Tree/snapshot** — it's there, just not obvious:
```rust
// The full file tree at this commit
let tree: MergedTree = commit.tree();

// Individual file content
let value = tree.path_value(&repo_path)?;

// List all files
let entries = tree.entries();  // TreeEntriesIterator
```

**Diffs/changes** — compare the commit's tree against its parent tree:
```rust
let parent_tree = commit.parent_tree(repo).await?;
let commit_tree = commit.tree();

let mut diff_stream = parent_tree.diff_stream(&commit_tree, &EverythingMatcher);
while let Some(entry) = diff_stream.next().await {
    // entry.path — the file path
    // entry.values — Diff { before, after } of MergedTreeValue
}
```

So the full `Commit` public API relevant to you:

| Method | Returns | Notes |
|--------|---------|-------|
| `id()` | `&CommitId` | sync |
| `change_id()` | `&ChangeId` | sync |
| `description()` | `&str` | sync |
| `author()` / `committer()` | `&Signature` | sync |
| `parent_ids()` | `&[CommitId]` | sync, just IDs |
| `parents()` | `Vec<Commit>` | async |
| `tree()` | `MergedTree` | the snapshot |
| `tree_ids()` | `&Merge<TreeId>` | may be conflicted |
| `parent_tree(repo)` | `MergedTree` | async, for diffing |
| `is_empty(repo)` | `bool` | no changes vs parent |
| `has_conflict()` | `bool` | merge conflicts |

The asymmetry makes sense — parents are stored in the commit data (O(1) lookup), but children require scanning the DAG (O(n) or index lookup). That's why children go through revsets or the index, not the `Commit` type.

> DEVELOPER

If I have a Commit then there is a complete snapshot of the tree at that point and if I want the difference between it and a parent I have to get the snapshot of the parent and then ask for a diff?

> AGENT

Yes, exactly. Each commit stores a full tree snapshot (not a delta). The diff is computed on the fly by comparing two snapshots. There's no stored delta.

The `parent_tree(repo)` method is a convenience that handles merge commits (merges multiple parent trees) so you don't have to. But fundamentally it's just: get tree A, get tree B, compare.

> DEVELOPER

What is stored on "disk"?

> AGENT

Since jj uses git as its backend (in your colocated setup), what's on disk is **git objects** — which are content-addressable and deduplicated:

- **Blobs** — file contents (deduplicated by SHA)
- **Trees** — directory listings (pointers to blobs and sub-trees)
- **Commits** — pointer to a root tree + parent commit IDs + metadata

So each commit points to a full tree, but the tree is composed of shared objects. If a file didn't change between commits, both trees point to the same blob. Git also packs objects into packfiles with delta compression for storage efficiency.

The "full snapshot" is logical, not physical. When you call `commit.tree()`, jj walks the tree objects to reconstruct the full state. When you diff two trees, it can short-circuit — if two subtrees have the same hash, skip them entirely. Only the differing paths need to be traversed.

So the layers are:

| Level | What it looks like | What's on disk |
|-------|-------------------|----------------|
| API | full snapshot per commit | shared, deduplicated objects |
| Storage | — | content-addressable blobs + trees |
| Packfile | — | delta-compressed for space |

jj adds its own operation store (`.jj/op_store/`) for tracking operations and predecessor history, but the actual commit/tree/blob data lives in `.git/objects/`.

> DEVELOPER

So I as the user of jj-lib if I have a Commit and call `fn tree(&self) -> MergedTree` I get a snapshot and I see that `tree` invokes `MergedTree::new(..)` I assume there is a "cost" to doing a new and it's not 0, for instance linx uses a monorepo so a snapshot is going to be quite large, correct?

> AGENT

Not quite — `MergedTree::new()` is cheap. It just wraps the tree IDs that are already in the commit data. It's essentially storing a pointer (the tree hash), not materializing the tree.

The cost comes when you **traverse** the tree:

- `tree.entries()` — walks every directory and file. On a Linux monorepo, that's millions of entries. Expensive.
- `tree.path_value(&path)` — walks only the directories along that path. Cheap, O(depth).
- `tree.diff_stream(&other, &matcher)` — compares two trees, but **short-circuits on matching subtree hashes**. If only 3 files changed in a commit, it skips the millions of identical subtrees almost instantly.

So the mental model is:

| Operation | Cost | Why |
|-----------|------|-----|
| `commit.tree()` | near zero | just wraps tree IDs |
| `tree.path_value(path)` | O(path depth) | walks ~4-5 directory nodes |
| `tree.diff_stream(other)` | O(changed files) | skips identical subtrees by hash |
| `tree.entries()` | O(entire repo) | walks everything — avoid on large repos |

The git object store does lazy I/O — tree nodes are loaded from disk (or packfile) only when accessed. So you only pay for what you touch.

> DEVELOPER

Ok thinking of how gitk works we have the "patch" "tree" selector at the top of the lower right panel. In "patch" mode it lists the files that changed and simultaneiously shows a diff on the lower left panel, that could be kinda expensive as we have two "snapshots" and have to do a diff.

> AGENT

Right, but it's the cheap kind of expensive. The diff only visits the subtrees where hashes differ — on a Linux monorepo commit that touches 5 files, it might load maybe 20-30 tree objects to find those 5 differences out of millions. The hash comparison skips entire subtrees instantly.

The actual expensive part is reading the **blob contents** to produce the line-by-line diff display. But even that is only for the files that changed, not the whole repo.

The "tree" mode in gitk is the potentially scary one — that's browsing the full snapshot. But even there, gitk lazily expands directories on click, so it only loads tree objects as you navigate into them.

So gitk's approach is efficient by design:
- **Patch mode**: diff_stream (cheap) + read changed blobs (proportional to change size)
- **Tree mode**: lazy directory expansion (load on demand)

Neither ever materializes the full snapshot into memory. The content-addressable object store makes this natural — everything is a pointer until you dereference it.

> DEVELOPER

Is there any reason to believe jj-lib is not implemented as efficently and this may not work well using it, or maybe the backend is actually the same git code used by gitk?

> AGENT

They're not the same code but they use the same data. jj-lib reads the same `.git/objects/` and packfiles, but with its own Rust implementation — it doesn't shell out to git or link libgit2 for tree operations.

The key question is whether jj-lib's tree diff does the same hash-based short-circuiting. Let me check.

> AGENT

The key line is **604**:

```rust
if root_diff.is_changed() && !matcher.visit(root_dir).is_nothing() {
```

And in `merged_tree_entry_diff` (line 638), it compares entries name-by-name at each directory level. The short-circuit is implicit — `is_changed()` checks if the `MergedTreeValue` (which contains tree hashes) differs. If two subtrees have the same hash, there's no diff entry, so that subtree is never descended into.

So yes, **jj-lib does the same hash-based short-circuiting** as git. It's a different implementation (Rust, not C) reading the same on-disk objects, but the algorithm is equivalent: compare tree hashes, only recurse into directories that differ.

It also has a concurrency feature git/gitk doesn't — line 266 shows it can use `TreeDiffStreamImpl` with concurrent I/O for loading tree objects in parallel when the backend supports it.

No reason to believe it would be less efficient than gitk for this. If anything, the async concurrent tree loading could make it faster on large repos.

   230→
   231→### gitk: still the best for DAG traversal
   232→
   233→gitk's killer feature: the parent/child list lets you **click-to-jump**
   234→to any commit, even if it's far off-screen. True graph traversal, not
   235→scrolling. Works in colocated jj repos via `jj git export`.
   236→
   237→**Downsides:** Shows Git's view only — no change IDs, no revsets, no
   238→jj bookmarks. Detached HEAD display is confusing. Cannot perform jj
   239→operations. Does not work with non-colocated jj repos.
   240→
   241→### gg-cli: close but not there
   242→
   243→Renders a true graphical DAG. Can show parents in the detail pane and
   244→click to select a parent — but **only if that parent is already visible
   245→in the left panel**. If the parent is off-screen, there is no jump-to
   246→behavior; you must manually scroll using the slider. This makes it
   247→**useless on large/complex repos** for DAG traversal.
   248→
   249→Drag-and-drop rebase is nice, but the lack of click-to-jump is a
   250→dealbreaker for navigation.
   251→
   252→### Other tools: list navigators, not graph navigators
   253→
   254→- **jj-fzf** — displays `jj log` graph in fzf with live revset editing.
   255→  Navigation is list-based (up/down), not graph-based (follow edges).
   256→- **jjui** — revision tree view, growing fast, but still fundamentally
   257→  a list navigator with a graph drawn alongside.
   258→- **lazyjj** — operations-focused TUI, not DAG-focused.
   259→- **jjdag** — pre-alpha, explicitly DAG-focused (Magit-style keys,
   260→  foldable tree). Worth watching.
   261→- **jj log** — revset language is powerful for querying (`children(x)`,
   262→  `ancestors(main..@)`) but output is static, not interactive.
   263→
   264→### Comparison
   265→
   266→| Tool     | DAG display | Click-to-jump parent/child | Native jj |
   267→|----------|-------------|---------------------------|-----------|
   268→| gitk     | graphical   | **yes** (both directions)  | no        |
   269→| gg-cli   | graphical   | parent only, no jump if off-screen | yes |
   270→| jj-fzf   | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |
   271→| jjui     | ASCII       | no                         | yes       |
   272→| jj log   | ASCII       | n/a (not interactive)      | yes       |
   273→
   274→### Conclusion
   275→
   276→**gitk remains the best tool for interactive DAG traversal** despite
   277→its jj blindspots. The jj ecosystem is missing a tool that combines
   278→jj-native concepts (change IDs, revsets, bookmarks) with gitk-style
   279→click-to-jump on parent/child links. This would be a strong feature
   280→request for gg-cli, which already has the infrastructure but lacks the
   281→jump-to behavior.
   282→
   283→### Cautionary tale: multi-tool dangers
   284→
   285→While testing jj-fzf and gg-cli simultaneously (with Zed editor also
   286→open), it's easy to accidentally move `@` to an unexpected commit via
   287→one tool without noticing. When Zed then sees files disappear from the
   288→working copy, closing a "changed" file and declining to save effectively
   289→deletes it. Recovery: `jj new main` to repoint `@`, or
   290→`jj file show <path> -r <rev> --at-op <op>` to extract from history.
   291→

   294→- `src/revset.rs` — revset expression types, builders, parsing
   295→- `src/evolution.rs` — evolog/predecessor traversal
   296→- `src/graph.rs` — graph structures, TopoGroupedGraphIterator
   297→- `src/dag_walk.rs` — generic DAG traversal (DFS, topo sort)
   298→- `src/repo.rs` — Repo trait, ReadonlyRepo
   299→- `src/view.rs` — View (heads, bookmarks, git refs)
   300→- `src/index.rs` — Index trait (ancestry, common ancestors)
   301→- `src/commit.rs` — Commit wrapper (parent_ids, change_id, description)
   302→- `src/backend.rs` — backend data types (CommitId, ChangeId, Signature)
   303→- `src/op_store.rs` — operation storage (commit_predecessors)
   304→- `src/operation.rs` — Operation type (predecessors_for_commit)
   305→

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1/notes/chores-02.md",
  "old_string": "### Key jj-lib source files (0.39.0)",
  "new_string": "## jj-lib Commit API and tree storage model (2026-03-18)\n\n### What's on the Commit type\n\nGiven a `Commit` (from `repo.store().get_commit(&commit_id)?`), the\npublic API includes:\n\n| Method | Returns | Cost |\n|--------|---------|------|\n| `id()` | `&CommitId` | zero — stored in struct |\n| `change_id()` | `&ChangeId` | zero |\n| `description()` | `&str` | zero |\n| `author()` / `committer()` | `&Signature` | zero |\n| `parent_ids()` | `&[CommitId]` | zero — stored in commit data |\n| `parents()` | `Vec<Commit>` | async, loads parent commits |\n| `tree()` | `MergedTree` | near-zero — wraps tree IDs, no I/O |\n| `tree_ids()` | `&Merge<TreeId>` | zero |\n| `parent_tree(repo)` | `MergedTree` | async, merges parent trees |\n| `is_empty(repo)` | `bool` | compares tree to parent tree |\n| `has_conflict()` | `bool` | checks if tree IDs are conflicted |\n\n### What's NOT on Commit\n\n- **Children** — parent→child is a reverse lookup. Requires revsets:\n  ```rust\n  resolve_revset(&workspace, &repo, &format!(\"children({})\", change_id))?\n  ```\n  Parents are stored in the commit; children require scanning the DAG.\n\n- **Diff/changes** — no method returns \"what changed in this commit.\"\n  Must be computed by comparing two tree snapshots.\n\n### Getting diffs between a commit and its parent\n\n```rust\nuse jj_lib::matchers::EverythingMatcher;\n\nlet parent_tree = commit.parent_tree(repo).await?;\nlet commit_tree = commit.tree();\n\nlet mut diff_stream = parent_tree.diff_stream(&commit_tree, &EverythingMatcher);\nwhile let Some(entry) = diff_stream.next().await {\n    // entry.path  — RepoPathBuf (the file that changed)\n    // entry.values — Diff { before, after } of MergedTreeValue\n}\n```\n\n`parent_tree()` handles merge commits by merging multiple parent trees.\n\n### What's stored on disk\n\njj uses git as its backend. On disk: git's content-addressable objects.\n\n- **Blobs** — file contents, deduplicated by SHA\n- **Trees** — directory listings (pointers to blobs and sub-trees)\n- **Commits** — pointer to root tree + parent IDs + metadata\n\nEach commit points to a **full tree snapshot**, but the tree is composed\nof shared objects. If a file didn't change, both commits' trees point to\nthe same blob. Git also delta-compresses objects in packfiles.\n\nThe \"full snapshot\" is **logical, not physical**. `commit.tree()` just\nwraps the root tree hash — no I/O happens until you traverse.\n\n### Cost model for tree operations\n\n| Operation | Cost | Why |\n|-----------|------|-----|\n| `commit.tree()` | near zero | wraps tree IDs, no I/O |\n| `tree.path_value(&path)` | O(path depth) | walks ~4-5 tree nodes |\n| `tree.diff_stream(&other)` | O(changed subtrees) | skips identical subtrees by hash |\n| `tree.entries()` | O(entire repo) | walks everything — avoid on large repos |\n\nThe diff algorithm compares tree entries level by level. If a subtree\nhash matches, it's skipped entirely — never descended into. On a Linux\nmonorepo commit that touches 5 files, it loads ~20-30 tree objects out\nof millions.\n\nThe expensive part of a gitk-style patch view is reading **blob\ncontents** for the line-by-line diff, but only for changed files.\n\n### jj-lib vs gitk efficiency\n\njj-lib is a separate Rust implementation reading the same `.git/objects/`\nand packfiles — it does NOT shell out to git or link libgit2.\n\nThe tree diff uses the **same hash-based short-circuiting** as git:\n`TreeDiffIterator` (in `merged_tree.rs`) checks `is_changed()` on\n`MergedTreeValue` (which compares tree hashes) and only recurses into\ndirectories that differ.\n\njj-lib also has a feature gitk lacks: **concurrent tree loading**. When\n`store.concurrency() > 1`, it uses `TreeDiffStreamImpl` with async\nparallel I/O for loading tree objects, potentially faster than gitk on\nlarge repos.\n\nSource: `jj-lib-0.39.0/src/merged_tree.rs`, lines 260-690.\n\n### Key jj-lib source files (0.39.0)"
}

     7→use std::process::ExitCode;
     8→
     9→use clap::{Parser, Subcommand};
    10→
    11→#[derive(Parser, Debug)]
    12→#[command(version, about = "vc-x1: jj workspace tooling")]
    13→pub struct Cli {
    14→    #[command(subcommand)]
    15→    command: Commands,
    16→}
    17→
    18→#[derive(Subcommand, Debug)]
    19→enum Commands {
    20→    /// Print the changeID for a revision
    21→    Chid(chid::ChidArgs),
    22→
    23→    /// Show full description of a commit
    24→    Desc(desc::DescArgs),
    25→
    26→    /// List commits in a jj repo
    27→    List(list::ListArgs),
    28→
    29→    /// Squash working copy into target commit
    30→    Finalize(finalize::FinalizeArgs),
    31→}
    32→
    33→fn main() -> ExitCode {
    34→    let cli = Cli::parse();
    35→
    36→    match cli.command {
    37→        Commands::Chid(args) => {
    38→            if let Err(e) = chid::chid(&args) {
    39→                eprintln!("error: {e}");
    40→                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
    41→            }
    42→            ExitCode::SUCCESS
    43→        }
    44→        Commands::Desc(args) => {
    45→            if let Err(e) = desc::desc(&args) {
    46→                eprintln!("error: {e}");
    47→                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
    48→            }
    49→            ExitCode::SUCCESS
    50→        }
    51→        Commands::List(args) => {
    52→            if let Err(e) = list::list(&args) {
    53→                eprintln!("error: {e}");
    54→                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
    55→            }
    56→            ExitCode::SUCCESS
    57→        }
    58→        Commands::Finalize(args) => {
    59→            let opts = args.into_opts();
    60→            finalize::log_msg(&opts.log, "main: finalize entry");
    61→            match finalize::finalize(&opts) {
    62→                Ok(()) => {
    63→                    finalize::log_msg(&opts.log, "main: finalize exit ok");
    64→                    ExitCode::SUCCESS
    65→                }
    66→                Err(e) => {
    67→                    finalize::log_msg(&opts.log, &format!("main: finalize exit err={e}"));
    68→                    eprintln!("error: {e}");
    69→                    ExitCode::FAILURE
    70→                }
    71→            }
    72→        }
    73→    }
    74→}

   452→    #[test]
   453→    fn unknown_command() {
   454→        let err = parse_err(&["vc-x1", "bogus"]);
   455→        assert!(err.contains("bogus"));
   456→    }
   457→}
